We find that forced-choice behavior aligns substantially more with hidden-state geometry than free association. In a held-out-words regression, behavioral similarity (especially forced choice) predicts unseen hidden-state similarities beyond lexical baselines and cross-model consensus, indicating that behavior-only measurements retain recoverable information about internal semantic geometry. Finally, we discuss implications for the ability of behavioral tasks to uncover hidden cognitive states.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published at the 43rd International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML 2026), Seoul, South Korea. 27 pages, 16 figures, 6 tables. Code: https://github.com/schiekiera/llm-association-geometry. Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/schiekiera/llm-association-geometry</p></details> |
| **[SchemaWeaver: Accelerating On-Device Mobile Agentic AI via Schema-Guided Parallel LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17436v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) on mobile devices can act as agents that call external software tools to complete complex tasks, but the major challenge of supporting on-device mobile agentic AI with LLMs is the long latency of LLM tool calling on mobile devices. In particular, the decode phase is the main contributor of such long latency, but is hard to speed up on mobile processors as it is bound by memory bandwidth rather than compute power. To address this limitation, in this paper we present SchemaWeaver, a new on-device mobile agentic AI technique that enforces schema-guided parallel decoding in the decode phase of LLM tool calling, to reduce its latency by producing multiple tokens in each decode pass. SchemaWeaver can be generically applied to the LLM inference engine on mobile devices without involving any training efforts or changes on LLMs, and our experiment results on major mobile SoCs and LLM families show that SchemaWeaver can reduce the LLM tool calling latency by up to 3.9 times, compared to the competitive baselines.</p></details> | 16 pages, 14 figures |
| **[VeriRouter: Towards Verifiable LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17431v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) routers are increasingly deployed to optimize quality, latency, and cost through dynamic model selection and request rewriting. However, a compromised router can cause ineligible selection on the control plane, unendorsed rewriting or unfaithful relay on the data plane, undermining LLM routing integrity. We present VeriRouter, a verifiable LLM routing framework. Under an endorsed public context binding a private semantic index and rewrite banks, VeriRouter combines ZK proofs with a provider-issued serving certificate to establish integrity across both planes while keeping the router's private routing assets hidden from clients. For model selection, we design an efficient ZK proof for semantic search. For rewriting, local checks enforce message preservation and additions from committed banks. On real-world workloads, VeriRouter's model-selection and rewrite proofs average 21.92~s and 4.24~s, yielding 1,307$\times$ and 101$\times$ speedups over the fastest completing baselines. Relative to serving without verification, VeriRouter adds only 4.08 s under per-turn selection and 0.11 s under session-pinned selection.</p></details> |  |
| **[Reading the Room: Foundations, Design, and Challenges of Normative Competence in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10906v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human communities are governed by normative systems: shared standards that produce \textit{norms} dictating acceptable behavior, enforced through community sanctioning. Aligning increasingly autonomous AI systems with these norms is a central alignment challenge, complicated by the fact that norms are vast in number, change quickly, and are often arbitrary (e.g., dress or language conventions). Thus, alignment requires \textit{normative competence}: the ability to discern from interaction alone what norms a community enforces without relying on static pretrained knowledge. We introduce a multi-agent community debate setting, where access to debate is governed by synthetic norms, to study normative competence in isolation from pretraining exposure. We show that baseline LLM agents fail to learn norms even when doing so would improve their accuracy. We then experiment with various \textit{normative modules} -- architectural components for norm inference -- finding that norm-following is highly sensitive to both the style of norm and the model powering the normative module, suggesting a lack of generalizability. Furthermore, when idiosyncratic, non-normative behaviors accompany the true norm, LLM agents exhibit an unselective attribution failure: they indiscriminately copy idiosyncratic noise alongside enforced rules, a pattern that persists even when imitating unnecessary behaviors is explicitly penalized. To the best of our knowledge, our work is the first to operationalize and evaluate normative competence in LLMs, demonstrating that current AI systems excel at behavioral mimicry but lack the capacity to discern socially enforced order.</p></details> |  |
| **[EPOCH: Coverage-Aware Evolution with Experiential Preference Optimization for LLM-based Automated Heuristic Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17379v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based automated heuristic design (AHD) discovers heuristics for combinatorial optimization problems (COPs), where an LLM generates candidates in an evolutionary search. Existing frameworks freeze the generator or fine-tune it on a scalar fitness signal hiding which instances each heuristic covers. Reflection-based frameworks improve heuristics with natural-language feedback, but it stays in the prompt and never reaches the generator's weights. We propose EPOCH, which makes instance coverage the objective of the evolutionary search and fine-tunes the generator to internalize reflection. Parent selection, operator allocation, and stagnation recovery all optimize how well the heuristics jointly cover the training instances, so the search returns a complementary heuristic ensemble. The generator learns from its own search by experiential preference optimization. A teacher LLM returns a natural-language insight on the generated heuristics, and the generator produces new heuristics with this insight. These improvements join the preference pairs for direct preference optimization (DPO). Since DPO only teaches the generator to rank these improvements, a reflective self-distillation (RSD) term makes it more likely to generate them on its own, and the gains accumulate over the long horizon of the search. Across COPs and open-source LLM backbones, EPOCH's heuristic ensembles outperform inference-only and fine-tuned baselines and generalize across problem scales.</p></details> |  |
| **[NeuroFilter: Activation-Based Guardrails for Privacy-Conscious LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.14660v3)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic Large Language Models (LLMs) are models able to reason, plan, and execute tools over unstructured data. These abilities enable transformative applications in domains spanning personal assistants, financial, and legal domains, etc. While these systems can substantially improve productivity and service quality, effective agency typically requires access to sensitive personal or organizational information. However, this access introduces critical inference-time privacy risks, specifically regarding contextually appropriate information disclosure. While recent studies highlight the inability of agentic LLMs to consistently adhere to privacy norms, existing defenses often rely on auxiliary LLM-based monitors. However, these defenses are expensive and offer limited protection against attacks that are robust to semantic censorship. To contrast this background, this paper proposes a notion of privacy filters based on activation probing. We show that these filters are both computationally efficient and effective for both single-turn and multi-turn conversational settings. Furthermore, this work provides the first systematic investigation into probing model internals across a conversation trajectory, moving beyond static, single-prompt analysis to capture the evolving state of privacy-sensitive interactions, illustrating their applicability across contexts, attack styles, multiple base models, and challenging settings such as mosaic attacks, while providing nuanced insights into the nature of the discovered concepts and modular construction of probes.</p></details> |  |
| **[GPU Goldmines: Tunable WebGPU Kernels for Edge LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17321v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As demand for LLM inference grows and consumer GPUs grow more capable, edge deployment is becoming increasingly attractive. WebGPU is compelling in this setting because it offers cross-platform portability and browser accessibility. However, these same strengths also make high performance difficult to achieve, as an implementation that performs well in one context may not perform well in another. To address this, we present GPU Goldmines, a framework for specifying, executing, and tuning WebGPU kernels. We instantiate GPU Goldmines with kernels for key LLM building blocks: matrix multiplication, matrix-vector multiplication, and flash attention. We characterize performance across diverse devices, shapes, and browsers, and use the resulting database to accelerate subsequent autotuning and configuration selection. On shapes drawn from Llama and Qwen models, our matrix multiplication kernels outperform ONNX Runtime and llama.cpp WebGPU kernels by 7.0x and 1.41x geometric mean, respectively, and deliver end-to-end improvements of 1.48x in the prefill stage of llama.cpp for chat workloads.</p></details> | <details><summary>11 pa...</summary><p>11 pages, 5 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[When Agents Commit Too Soon: Diagnosing Premature Commitment in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22936v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using LLM agents make early choices about what to search for, which evidence to trust, and what to do next. A wrong early choice can steer every later tool call, so an agent may produce a coherent but incorrect result. Existing evaluation does not reveal this process: final-answer scoring comes only after completion, and agreement across runs can be high even when every run is wrong. We ask whether hidden states reveal that repeated runs have already settled into stable behavior. Our contributions are threefold. First, we introduce representational commitment, which measures hidden-state similarity across repeated runs at the same step. On Llama-3.1-70B running ReAct on HotpotQA, we show that higher step-4 similarity is associated with lower whole-trajectory variability (r = -0.35); we find the same pattern in action sequences and replicate it on two other models and StrategyQA. This provides an internal measure for studying when agent behavior stabilizes. Second, we establish the measure's scope. Shared evidence explains much of the observed association, the HotpotQA result disappears on hard questions, and correct and confidently wrong questions overlap. We therefore show that the measure detects stability, not correctness, and cannot by itself establish that commitment was premature. Third, we evaluate monitoring and intervention. A monitor built from three partial runs predicts whole-trajectory variability (mean AUROC 0.81). A commitment prompt reduces variability by 28% relative to a token-matched filler, but not significantly relative to the standard agent after correction, and it does not improve accuracy. Together, these contributions provide a measurement and evaluation framework for studying how agent trajectories stabilize and for testing when additional evidence, sampling, or review may be useful.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 16 figures. Camera-ready version, Workshop on Agent Behavior at COLM 2026</p></details> |
| **[TRAP: Tree-Guided Reinforcement-Augmented Probing for Automated LLM Jailbreaking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17313v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Aligned large language models (LLMs) remain vulnerable to jailbreak prompts that circumvent their safety guardrails, making the identification of these vulnerabilities important for understanding and mitigating model weaknesses. Existing automated jailbreak methods explore prompt candidates or reuse attack experience, but generally do not learn over a persistent hierarchy that connects related jailbreak strategies, such as role-play, encoding, and obfuscation. This leaves the relationships among reusable strategies underused in guiding adaptive attack selection. We present Tree-Guided Reinforcement-Augmented Probing (TRAP), a hierarchical framework for automated jailbreak strategy discovery that requires only black-box access to the target LLM. TRAP organizes established jailbreak methods and successful attack templates into a semantic strategy tree, connecting broad attack approaches to concrete, reusable strategies. A reinforcement-learning policy based on a tree-structured gradient bandit explores this tree and uses evaluator feedback to update strategy preferences along each selected path. The policy retains these preferences across attack objectives, allowing experience from earlier attempts to guide subsequent strategy selection. Retrieval-augmented prompt construction translates the selected strategy into an attack for the current objective using relevant successful examples. Experiments on AdvBench across seven aligned LLMs show that TRAP outperforms its counterparts on six models under the reported method settings and the common primary evaluator, achieving attack success rates of 45.6% to 97.9% on these models. These findings support hierarchical strategy learning as an effective approach to discovering jailbreak vulnerabilities in aligned LLMs.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages. Code: https://github.com/Rezamirbagheri110/TRAP</p></details> |
